If you are applying to the ARC stream, read AlgZoo (https://www.alignment.org/blog/algzoo-uninterpreted-models-with-fewer-than-1-500-parameters/) and enter the White-Box Estimation Challenge (https://www.alignment.org/blog/announcing-the-arc-white-box-estimation-challenge/) — a leaderboard entry is a better application artifact than an essay.

## 11. Governance, safety cases, and technical governance

### Governance, Safety Cases, and Technical Governance

*All framework versions, dates and legal statuses below were verified against primary sources on 2026-09-05. This section ages fastest; check versions before quoting.*

### 1. The governance problem from first principles

Frontier AI governance exists because of an information asymmetry plus a coordination failure. The asymmetry: only the developer knows what its model can do before release, and often not even then — capability is discovered, not designed. The coordination failure: any single developer that slows down to build safeguards loses ground to one that doesn't, so the safety floor is set by the least careful lab rather than the most careful one.

Voluntary frameworks — Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy, OpenAI's Preparedness Framework, Google DeepMind's Frontier Safety Framework — were the industry's answer: pre-commit publicly to capability thresholds and to mitigations that trigger when you cross them. The structure is genuinely useful. It converts "are we being safe?" into "have we crossed threshold X, and if so have we implemented mitigation Y?", which is auditable in a way that vibes are not.

The fragility is not a critic's inference; it is written into the policies. Anthropic's RSP v3.0 (February 2026) restructured the entire document around exactly this problem, splitting "our plan as a company" from "ambitious industry-wide recommendations," and stating plainly: *"we cannot unilaterally and unconditionally commit to staying in line with the industry-wide recommendations."* The stated reason is the collective action problem — "if one AI developer paused development to implement safety measures while others moved forward... the developers with the weakest protections would set the pace." The same document concludes that *"the best way for these recommendations to be implemented is likely via governance of all relevant frontier AI developers by third parties."* A leading lab's flagship safety policy is an argument for external regulation.

Three further fragilities are structural. **Self-definition**: the developer writes the threshold. **Self-measurement**: the developer runs the evals, and elicitation effort is a free parameter — under-elicit and you pass. **Self-grading**: the developer decides whether the evidence clears the bar. Anthropic's RSP explicitly moved *away* from pre-specified evaluations toward "an affirmative case that models are sufficiently far from Capability Thresholds," trading auditability for flexibility.

Regulation can force disclosure, standardise incident reporting, create whistleblower channels, grant inspection powers, and make lying about your own framework legally costly. It cannot specify what a safe model is, because nobody knows. Every serious regime therefore lands in the same place — *make an argument that you are safe, and let someone else check it*. That is the safety case, and it is why a technical researcher matters here: the arguments are technical, and today they are weak.

### 2. The current state, as of 2026

Two structural facts dominate the 2026 picture. First, a Critical threshold has now actually been crossed: **GPT-6 Astra (released 3 September 2026) is the first model any developer has publicly assessed as crossing a Critical-equivalent threshold** — Critical for cybersecurity under OpenAI's Preparedness Framework, with Biological/Chemical at High. Second, governments have started writing capability thresholds of their own — **EO 14409** directs NSA/CISA/Treasury/NIST to build a *classified* cyber-capability benchmarking process and to designate "covered frontier models," arriving in the same quarter as Astra and entirely unconnected to it.

**Frontier safety frameworks**

| Developer | Current framework | Version / date | Risk domains | Threshold structure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Anthropic | Responsible Scaling Policy | **v3.4, effective 8 Jul 2026** (v3.0 rewrite 24 Feb 2026) | Non-novel chem/bio weapons; novel chem/bio weapons; misaligned AI in high-stakes settings (sabotage); automated AI R&D | Named capability/usage thresholds + "ASL-3 protections" as a mitigation package; adds Frontier Safety Roadmaps and published Risk Reports with external review |
| OpenAI | Preparedness Framework | **v2, 15 Apr 2025**; Frontier Governance Framework (28 May 2026) as a public compliance layer | Tracked: Biological & Chemical, Cybersecurity, AI Self-improvement; plus Research Categories (e.g. Long-range Autonomy) | **High** and **Critical**. High → safeguards before external deployment; Critical → *"safeguards even during the development of the covered system, irrespective of deployment plans"* |
| Google DeepMind | Frontier Safety Framework | **v3.1, 17 Apr 2026** (v3.0 22 Sep 2025) | CBRN, cyber, ML R&D acceleration, misalignment/undermining control, **harmful manipulation** (new in v3) | Critical Capability Levels (CCLs) + new **Tracked Capability Levels (TCLs)**; safety case reviews before external launch and for large-scale internal deployment |
| xAI | Frontier AI Framework (FAIF) | **effective 30 Jun 2026** | CBRN, Offensive Cybersecurity, Loss of Control, Harmful Manipulation | Borrows terminology from the EU GPAI Code of Practice; references NIST AI RMF and ISO/IEC 42001 |
| Meta | Frontier AI Framework | Published; **not** among EU GPAI Code signatories `[partly unverified]` | — | — |

METR's *Common Elements of Frontier AI Safety Policies* (16 Dec 2025) counts twelve companies with published frameworks — Anthropic, OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Magic, Naver, Meta, G42, Cohere, Microsoft, Amazon, xAI, NVIDIA — and distils nine shared components (capability thresholds, weight security, deployment mitigations, halting conditions for deployment and development, full capability elicitation, evaluation timing, accountability, update procedures).

**Which thresholds have been crossed**

| Model | Assessment | Source |
|---|---|---|
| GPT-6 Astra (3 Sep 2026) | **Critical — Cybersecurity**; **High** — Bio/Chem (explicitly *not* Critical). Safeguards applied to internal deployment: encrypted checkpoints, universal misalignment monitoring that pages humans, blocking alignment evals, staged internal rollout | OpenAI system card |
| Claude Opus 5 (Jul 2026) | *"deployed with ASL-3 protections, treating it as having CB-1 capabilities. Autonomy threat model 1 is applicable."* Does not cross the automated AI R&D threshold | Anthropic Transparency Hub, 17 Aug 2026 |
| Claude Opus 4.6 (Feb 2026) | Does not cross AI R&D-4; released under ASL-3 (pre-v3.0 terminology) | Anthropic |

Note the terminology trap: Anthropic's v3 RSP kept "ASL-3 protections" as the *name of a mitigation package* while dropping numbered ASL capability levels in favour of named threat models (the hub's "CB-1" appears to correspond to the non-novel chem/bio row) — the industry-wide recommendations are "structured around requiring analysis and arguments making a strong case for safety, rather than AI Safety Levels."

| **International AI Safety Report 2026** | **Published 3 Feb 2026**, 2nd edition, chaired by Bengio, 100+ experts, 30+ countries/IGOs | Scientific synthesis; Extended Summary for Policymakers; Key Updates between editions |

### 3. Safety cases

A safety case is an artefact imported from aviation, nuclear, rail and automotive assurance: **a claim, the evidence, and an explicit argument connecting them**. Not "we ran evals and they looked fine," but "here is the top-level claim (this deployment will not cause a catastrophe), here is its decomposition into sub-claims, here is the evidence for each, and here are the defeaters we considered."

Clymer, Gabrieli, Krueger and Larsen (arXiv:2403.10462, March 2024) give the canonical taxonomy of four argument types, in increasing order of how much you must trust the model:

1. **Inability** — "total inability to cause a catastrophe." Evidence: capability evaluations with strong elicitation, plus an argument that elicitation was adequate and that the eval-to-deployment gap is conservative. UK AISI and GovAI published a template for exactly this shape (offensive cyber inability, Nov 2024).
2. **Control** — "sufficiently strong control measures." Evidence: control evaluations, where a red team deliberately builds a model that *tries* to subvert monitoring in a proxy deployment environment. Korbak, Clymer, Hilton, Shlegeris and Irving (arXiv:2501.17315) sketch this for an internally deployed agent exfiltrating weights; the case hinges on three claims — the red team adequately elicits the capability, control measures remain at least as effective in real deployment as in the eval, and developers extrapolate conservatively.
3. **Trustworthiness** — "trustworthiness despite capability to cause harm." Evidence: an alignment argument. Buhl, Pfau, Hilton and Irving (arXiv:2505.03989) sketch one based on debate, resting on four assumptions: the agent is good at the debate game; good debate performance implies mostly-honesty; honesty persists through deployment; the deployment context tolerates some errors. They are explicit that open research problems must be solved before this is compelling.
4. **Deference** — "deference to credible AI advisors," if systems become powerful enough that we defer to their judgment. Nobody has a real version of this.

**The 2026 convergence.** All three major frameworks have quietly become safety-case regimes. Anthropic's RSP now requires "an affirmative case that models are sufficiently far from Capability Thresholds" instead of pre-specified evals, and publishes externally reviewed Risk Reports. DeepMind's FSF conducts "safety case reviews prior to external launches when relevant CCLs are reached," extended in v3.1 to large-scale internal ML R&D deployments. The EU GPAI Code of Practice's Commitments 3, 4 and 7 (systemic risk analysis, **acceptance determination**, and Model Reports) are a safety case in regulatory clothing.

**Where they currently fail.** This is the most useful part to know.

- **They have barely survived contact with external review.** Barrett et al. (arXiv:2604.21964, April 2026) applied the Assurance 2.0 methodology to DeepMind's published scheming-inability safety case and "surface[d] substantive new concerns that materially affect the scope of the safety case and its applicability for decision-making," concluding that developer-authored cases suffer confirmation bias and conflicted incentives.
- **The methodology is immature relative to the assurance discipline it borrows from.** Feakins, Habli and Morgan (arXiv:2603.08760, March 2026) argue alignment-community safety-case work has "significant limitations" against established aerospace/nuclear practice.
- **Elicitation is the load-bearing weak point in every inability case.** OpenAI's Astra card concedes the mechanism: safety training causes refusals that *understate* capability, so — "consistent with previous system cards" — they use probes to detect refusals and count them as successes to get a conservative upper bound. Without that correction, safety training launders capability evals into false negatives.
- **Evaluation awareness and sandbagging break the eval-to-deployment inference.** The Astra card's own sections include "Awareness of CoT Monitoring Reduces Monitorability" and "Monitor Evasion Capability Under Prompted Sandbagging."
- **Control cases have no field data.** Nobody has run a control evaluation at real deployment scale against a genuinely scheming model.

### 4. Technical governance: where a technical researcher contributes

Reuel et al., *Open Problems in Technical AI Governance* (arXiv:2407.14981), organise the field as a matrix of **capacities** — Assessment, Access, Verification, Security, Operationalization, Ecosystem Monitoring — against **targets** — Data, Compute, Models & Algorithms, Deployment. Concrete problems, with their cell:

1. **Capability elicitation as a measurement science** *(Assessment × Models)*. Every inability safety case depends on an unfalsifiable claim that elicitation was adequate. Build elicitation-effort scaling laws: how does measured capability grow with scaffold quality, fine-tuning budget, and inference compute, and can you extrapolate the ceiling?
2. **Sandbagging and evaluation-awareness detection** *(Assessment × Models)*. Detect when a model recognises it is being evaluated and behaves differently — white-box interpretability with a direct governance consumer.
3. **Eval reproducibility and standardisation** *(Operationalization × Models)*. Published capability numbers do not reproduce across scaffolds, harnesses, and prompt formats. Versioned eval specifications, contamination detection, and reported uncertainty are unglamorous and urgently needed; the FMF's *Frontier Capability Assessments* is the current state of the art.
4. **Threshold operationalization** *(Operationalization × Deployment)*. Turn prose thresholds into measurable predicates. Anthropic's automated R&D threshold is "fully substitute for our entire set of Research Scientists and Research Engineers, at competitive costs (within a factor of 5)" — how would you *measure* that? EO 14409 requires the US government to do the same for cyber, classified.
5. **Control evaluations at scale** *(Assessment × Deployment)*. Red-team protocols, blue-team monitors, honest reporting of the safety/usefulness Pareto frontier. Redwood Research originated this line.
6. **Training-run and compute verification** *(Verification × Compute)*. Can a third party verify claims about how much compute was used, on what data, for what run, without seeing the weights? Sastry et al. (arXiv:2402.08797) frame compute as uniquely governable — detectable, excludable, quantifiable, concentrated in a short supply chain — and identify three capacities: visibility, allocation, enforcement, while flagging the risks (privacy, economic distortion, centralisation of power).
7. **On-chip governance mechanisms** *(Security × Compute)*. Location attestation, usage attestation, tamper-evident telemetry. No longer hypothetical: the Chip Security Act would direct Commerce to set standards for "chip security mechanisms," and the January 2026 BIS rule already conditions H200 exports on independent third-party performance testing. The flexHEG line of work sits here `[unverified — no primary flexHEG source retrieved this session]`.
8. **Model weight provenance and watermarking** *(Verification × Models)*. The EU's Article 50 transparency rules, live since 2 Aug 2026, require machine-readable marks on AI-generated content, with a Code of Practice signed by 180+ organisations. Robustness of these marks under adversarial removal is an open technical question.
9. **Structured access and third-party auditing infrastructure** *(Access × Models)*. What access does an external evaluator need — API, fine-tuning, logprobs, activations, helpful-only checkpoints, chain-of-thought? METR's Feb–Mar 2026 Frontier Risk Report was a pilot rogue-deployment assessment using "non-public information and model access"; a METR staffer spent three weeks red-teaming Anthropic's internal agent monitoring and found novel vulnerabilities. Building the secure-enclave and audit-log infrastructure that makes deep access safe to grant is a real engineering problem.
10. **Safe harbour for independent evaluation** *(Access × Deployment)*. Longpre et al. (arXiv:2403.04893) propose a **legal** safe harbour (indemnification for good-faith public-interest research) and a **technical** safe harbour (no account suspension). Neither is standard practice.
11. **Incident reporting standards** *(Ecosystem Monitoring × Deployment)*. SB 53 mandates 15-day reporting of critical safety incidents to California OES; the EU CoP has a serious-incident commitment; the FMF published *Information Sharing, Incident Reporting, and Incident Response for Frontier AI Risks* (12 May 2026). There is no shared taxonomy, severity scale, or interoperability. METR's July 2026 piece on post-incident propensity investigation is the closest thing to a forensic methodology.
12. **Chain-of-thought monitorability preservation** *(Assessment × Models)*. The FMF published a CoT Monitorability brief (27 Jan 2026); the Astra card reports monitorability degrading under adversarial conditions. Whether legible reasoning survives further optimisation pressure has direct governance consequences — every control safety case leans on it.

### 5. Established vs contested

**Broadly established.** Capability thresholds triggering mitigations are the right frame (all twelve published frameworks share it). Third-party pre-deployment evaluation adds information — OpenAI named UK AISI, Apollo Research, SecureBio and Irregular as external evaluators on Astra, and METR published external reviews of two Anthropic Risk Reports. Compute is the most governable input. Incident reporting should exist.

**Genuinely contested, with names.**

- **Open weights.** *Kapoor, Bommasani et al.* (arXiv:2403.07918) argue the debate has run without a marginal-risk framework and that open models' distinctive properties cut both ways; *Seger et al.* (arXiv:2311.09227) argue open-sourcing the most capable models is not obviously net-positive. The 2026 datapoint: UK AISI finds leading open-weight models (GLM-5.2, DeepSeek V4-Pro) match frontier closed models from 4–7 months earlier on cyber — narrowing the window in which closed-weight controls bind. Meta remains the loudest advocate and reportedly did not sign the EU Code.
- **Does regulation slow safety?** The White House position (EO 14365, March 2026 recommendations) is that state regulation creates a patchwork "thwarting" US competitiveness and that states should not regulate AI *development* at all. Anthropic's RSP takes the opposite view. SB 53 is the live test case, and the DOJ AI Litigation Task Force exists to challenge laws like it.
- **Voluntary frameworks: floor or fig leaf?** Anthropic's v3 restructuring explicitly *reduces* unilateral commitment in favour of competitor-contingent ones. Charitably, honesty about incentives; cynically, a ratchet loosening under competitive pressure. Be able to argue either.
- **Prescriptive evals vs affirmative cases.** Pre-specified evals are auditable but go stale and invite teaching to the test; affirmative cases are flexible but let the developer choose the argument. Anthropic moved from the first to the second in v3.0; the assurance community (Feakins et al., Barrett et al.) argues the second means nothing without external review.

### 6. Open problems attackable in a 10-week sprint

1. **Elicitation-gap benchmark.** Take one published dangerous-capability eval; measure how much the score moves with scaffolding, best-of-n, fine-tuning, and inference compute; report the curve. Nobody has a clean scaling law for "how wrong is an under-elicited eval," and every inability safety case needs one.
2. **Reproduce a published capability claim end-to-end.** Pick a number from a 2026 system card, rebuild the eval from its description, report the delta and what the description omitted. A directly publishable contribution to eval standardisation and an excellent MATS deliverable.
3. **Evaluation-awareness probe.** Train a probe for "the model believes it is being evaluated," validate on held-out eval and deployment-like distributions, and test whether behaviour conditions on the probe direction. Ties interpretability to a governance-legible claim.
4. **Structured-access threat model.** Specify the minimum access an external auditor needs for each of the four safety-case argument types, plus the controls that make each level grantable. Deliverable: a table mapping argument type → required access → containment mechanism.
5. **Incident taxonomy crosswalk.** Map SB 53's "critical safety incident" categories to the EU CoP serious-incident commitment and the FMF incident-reporting brief; produce a machine-readable schema and identify the reportable-in-one-but-not-the-other gaps. Desk work, immediately usable by regulators.
6. **Control evaluation on an open-weight agent.** Build a proxy deployment environment, a red-team policy, and a monitor; report the safety/usefulness Pareto frontier and how it degrades when the red team knows the monitor's prompt. The Korbak et al. structure at student scale.

### 7. Takes

**Take 1: The Astra Critical designation is the most important governance event of 2026, and almost nobody has updated on it.** For three years the frameworks were untested — thresholds defined for hypothetical future models. In September 2026 OpenAI declared a deployed model Critical on cyber — the first public Critical-equivalent designation by any developer — restricted it by default, and gated offensive use behind a vetted-defender program. The frameworks *fired*: real evidence they can bind. **Counterargument:** OpenAI also shipped the model. A threshold that triggers staged rollout rather than a pause is a disclosure regime, not a safety regime — and since the developer both defines and applies the threshold, "Critical" landing on a graduated commercial rollout may show the threshold was set where it would not hurt.

**Take 2: Elicitation, not interpretability, is the binding constraint on safety cases today.** Every inability and control argument reduces to "we tried hard enough to make the model do the bad thing and it couldn't." That claim is currently unfalsifiable, and it is where a strong ML engineer has the largest comparative advantage — it is ordinary, hard empirical ML work. **Counterargument:** elicitation matters only while inability arguments are viable. Once models are capable enough that inability fails everywhere, everything routes through control and trustworthiness, where interpretability is the bottleneck. If that transition is 2–3 years out, invest in interpretability now.

**Take 3: The US federal position and the leading labs' own policies point in opposite directions, and technical people under-appreciate this.** EO 14365 and the March 2026 recommendations say states should not regulate AI *development* and no new federal AI regulator should exist. Anthropic's RSP says the way out of the collective action problem is precisely third-party governance of all frontier developers. Meanwhile EO 14409 has the government building classified capability thresholds of its own. The US is simultaneously deregulating and building a shadow evaluation regime inside the national security state. **Counterargument:** that may be coherent rather than contradictory — keep commercial AI unregulated, handle catastrophic risk through national security channels. Defensible as design; just opaque, unreviewable, and useless for risks that are not cyber.

**Take 4: "Technical governance" is the highest-leverage under-supplied niche for a strong ML engineer.** Policy has more lawyers than measurement scientists, and every binding obligation now in law — EU Model Reports, SB 53 transparency reports, Anthropic Risk Reports, EO 14409's classified benchmark — is demand for people who can build reproducible evaluations and report honest uncertainty. **Counterargument:** it is career-risky. The work is less legible to ML hiring committees than a NeurIPS paper, funding concentrates in a few nonprofits, and if regulation is rolled back the demand evaporates. Do it because you can also do core empirical work, not instead of it.

### 8. Ranked reading list

**Top 5**

1. **Clymer, Gabrieli, Krueger, Larsen — "Safety Cases: How to Justify the Safety of Advanced AI Systems"** — https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.10462 — the four argument types. The single highest value-per-page item here.
2. **Anthropic RSP v3.4 (8 Jul 2026)** — https://www.anthropic.com/responsible-scaling-policy (changelog: https://www.anthropic.com/rsp-updates) — read the "Recommendations for Industry-Wide Safety" section for a lab arguing, in its own policy, for external regulation.
3. **Reuel et al. — "Open Problems in Technical AI Governance"** — https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.14981 — the capacity × target taxonomy. Use it to place any project you propose.
4. **OpenAI Preparedness Framework v2** — https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/18a02b5d-6b67-4cec-ab64-68cdfbddebcd/preparedness-framework-v2.pdf — plus the **GPT-6 Astra system card** at https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra — read the threshold definition, then read the model that crossed it.
5. **Korbak, Clymer, Hilton, Shlegeris, Irving — "A sketch of an AI control safety case"** — https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.17315 — the most concrete safety case anyone has written.

**Secondary**

- Sastry, Heim, Belfield, Anderljung, Brundage et al., "Computing Power and the Governance of AI" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.08797
- Barrett et al., "Lessons from External Review of DeepMind's Scheming Inability Safety Case" (Apr 2026) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21964
- Feakins, Habli, Morgan, "Clear, Compelling Arguments" (Mar 2026) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08760
- Buhl, Pfau, Hilton, Irving, debate safety case sketch — https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03989
- Longpre et al., "A Safe Harbor for AI Evaluation and Red Teaming" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.04893
- METR, "Common Elements of Frontier AI Safety Policies" — https://metr.org/common-elements
- UK AISI + GovAI, inability safety case template — https://aisi.gov.uk/work/safety-case-template-for-inability-arguments
- GPAI Code of Practice — https://code-of-practice.ai/ and https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/contents-code-gpai
- DeepMind FSF v3.1 — https://deepmind.google/blog/strengthening-our-frontier-safety-framework/
- International AI Safety Report 2026 — https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/
- SB 53 explainer (FPF) — https://fpf.org/blog/californias-sb-53-the-first-frontier-ai-law-explained/
- EO 14365 — https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/12/16/2025-23092/ensuring-a-national-policy-framework-for-artificial-intelligence ; EO 14409 — https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/06/05/2026-11415/promoting-advanced-artificial-intelligence-innovation-and-security
- Open-weights debate: Kapoor et al. https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.07918 ; Seger et al. https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.09227

**Institutional landscape, briefly.** Open Philanthropy renamed to **Coefficient Giving** (openphilanthropy.org 301-redirects to coefficientgiving.org); the **AI Safety Fund** runs under the Frontier Model Forum; governments fund UK AISI and CAISI. **METR** raised ~$71M in commitments in the six months to August 2026 and now externally reviews Anthropic's Risk Reports and runs pre-deployment evals of OpenAI models. Also: **Apollo Research** (scheming and deception evals), **Redwood Research** (AI control), **Transluce** (Docent; public oversight infrastructure), **FAR.AI**, **EleutherAI**, **Goodfire** (interpretability, now commercial with Silico), **ARC** (mechanistic explanation theory; Matching Sampling Principle, White-Box Estimation Challenge), **MIRI** (advocacy for delaying ASI), **CAIS**. **Timaeus has merged into Resolution.** The UK institute is the **AI Security Institute** (renamed 14 Feb 2025); the US institute is now **CAISI** at NIST.

ML experience is a bonus but not required."* — the least gatekept on ML pedigree. |
| 13 | [`andriushchenko-11`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/andriushchenko-11) | Maksym Andriushchenko (ELLIS Tübingen) | Automating AI research, **"measuring long-horizon agentic capabilities"**, new alignment methods. Publishes heavily. | *"Prior research experience… at least one completed project with first-author contribution"*; independent, strong CS + SWE background. |
| 14 | [`grosse-10`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/grosse-10) | Roger Grosse (Anthropic / U. Toronto) | Influence functions and training-data attribution applied to out-of-context reasoning and emergent misalignment. Adjacent to your probe work, different tool. | *"Experience working with LLM model internals. Strong background in statistics and/or applied math (esp. numerical linear algebra)."* Highest quantitative bar of the interp streams. |
| 15 | [`shard-11`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/shard-11) | Alex Turner, Alex Cloud (Anthropic / independent) | Shard theory: steering vectors, gradient routing, robust unlearning, unsupervised capability elicitation. Residual-stream intervention lineage. | ML/CS/stats background, ML engineering familiarity, strong programming, proven ML project experience. |

**Also worth a look:** [`africa`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/africa) (model motivations/character; unusually candid asks — *"Receptive to feedback… It's fine and often good to fail fast"*), [`oesterheld`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/oesterheld) (Redwood, conceptual reasoning), [`kwon`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/kwon) (secret loyalties — *"an LLM covertly tries to advance a principal's interests"*), [`sharkey-11`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/sharkey-11) (Goodfire, reverse-engineering methods), [`evans`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/evans) (Owain Evans / Truthful AI, deception detection).

**On Systems Security as a secondary:** it is smaller (6 mentors) and mostly *infrastructure* security, not ML research. [`thiergart-11`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/thiergart-11) (SL5 Task Force datacenter build) wants *"3+ years of Security Engineering, Infrastructure Engineering or related experience."* [`kulp-10`](https://www.matsprogram.org/stream/kulp-10) is GPU side-channel attacks but explicitly says *"experience with hardware is not a requirement… If in doubt: we encourage you to apply!"* Ticking Systems Security costs you one extra short-answer; it is cheap insurance but not your strong suit.

### Mentor advice, digested

#### Neel Nanda (GDM) — the clearest public statement of what good work in this area looks like

From his **prior-cycle** MATS application post ([neelnanda.io/blog/mats-apps-9](https://www.neelnanda.io/blog/mats-apps-9); the Winter 2027 equivalent returns 404 — treat wording as indicative, not current):

- **"My ideal application is one that teaches me something new."**
- **"I value clear writing, good taste (ie choosing interesting problems and making good decisions), technical skill, truth-seeking, skepticism and pragmatism."**
- The task shape: **"identifying an interpretability hypothesis, gathering evidence for and against it, and writing up the evidence and analysis clearly"** — a time-boxed ~12h (max 20) self-directed project.
- Unlike MATS's general application, he **encourages** LLM use as *"a crucial research tool."*

On process ([Explore, Understand, Distill](https://www.lesswrong.com/s/5GT3yoYM9gRmMEKqL/p/hjMy4ZxS5ogA9cTYK), [Key Mindsets](https://www.lesswrong.com/s/5GT3yoYM9gRmMEKqL/p/cbBwwm4jW6AZctymL), [Research Taste](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ldrss6o3tiKT6NdMm/my-research-process-understanding-and-cultivating-research)):

- **"Prioritise for information gain. Try to do a lot of experiments and frequently ask yourself 'am I getting enough information per unit time?'"**
- **"A great experiment elegantly, and conclusively distinguishes between several plausible hypotheses."**
- **"Fail fast. One of the largest time sinks possible is investing weeks to months into a failed direction."**
- **"Your ultimate goal is to find truth, not to produce an exciting paper."**

From [How To Become A Mechanistic Interpretability Researcher](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jP9KDyMkchuv6tHwm/how-to-become-a-mechanistic-interpretability-researcher):

- **"Every research result is false until proven otherwise. The more exciting a result is, the more likely it is to be false."**
- **"Before testing a hypothesis, set a five-minute timer and brainstorm, 'What are the ways this could be false?'"**
- **"Work in 1-2 week sprints, post-mortem after each, pivot to another project unless it's going great."**

From [Highly Opinionated Advice on How to Write ML Papers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eJGptPbbFPZGLpjsp/highly-opinionated-advice-on-how-to-write-ml-papers):

- **"Try to prioritise having at least one really compelling and hard to deny experiment, over a bunch of mediocre ones."**
- **"Assume you've made a mistake — what is that mistake?"**

#### Ethan Perez (Anthropic) — [Tips for Empirical Alignment Research](https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/dZFpEdKyb9Bf4xYn7/tips-for-empirical-alignment-research)

The most directly operational advice for your situation, and he co-leads the largest Empirical stream:

- **"reduce uncertainty at the fastest possible rate."**
- **"Send 10-100 messages to various models, to investigate the behavior in question or prototype your idea"** — before writing code.
- **"Tailor your experiments to take no longer than ~16h if you're running them frequently."** Shrink models and cut to *"1k or even 300"* examples.
- **"Every experiment is a win: What matters is whether or not you're learning about a problem."**
- On mentorship — read this as the MATS working contract: **"Overcommunicating with me is really helpful… Aim to post an update every full day of work you've done."**

His [Easy Paper Writing Tips](https://ethanperez.net/easy-paper-writing-tips/) is a mechanical checklist worth applying to any research prose: *"Put the verb as early in the sentence as possible"*; *"Never use passive tense; always specify the actor ('We find…')"*; *"Limit hedging"*; *"If a sentence is long, split it into two: one sentence, one idea."*

#### Buck Shlegeris / Redwood — the control agenda

- Definition ([The case for ensuring powerful AIs are controlled](https://www.greaterwrong.com/posts/kcKrE9mzEHrdqtDpE/the-case-for-ensuring-that-powerful-ais-are-controlled)): **"Control: Ensure that even if your models are scheming, you'll be safe, because they are not capable of subverting your safety measures."** Your continue/intervene/quit utility measurement is a control evaluation in exactly this sense — say so in that vocabulary.
- On scoping ([Redwood podcast](https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/the-inaugural-redwood-research-podcast)): **"I wish that we'd started out by trying to do a one-month-long project which had a tangible output."**
- **[Recent Redwood Research project proposals](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RRxhzshdpneyTzKfq/recent-redwood-research-project-proposals)** is the single most useful artifact for choosing a project a Redwood-adjacent mentor will like: *"All of these projects excite at least one Redwood researcher."* Covers monitoring protocols, untrusted monitoring/collusion, elicitation and sandbagging.
- On roles ([80,000 Hours](https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/buck-shlegeris-ai-control-scheming/)): **"a lot of people should not be trying to produce novel research; they should be trying to get implementations of stuff set."**

#### John Wentworth — [You Are Not Measuring What You Think You Are Measuring](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9kNxhKWvixtKW5anS/you-are-not-measuring-what-you-think-you-are-measuring)

Aim this directly at your probe results before you write the application:

- **"you are not measuring what you think you are measuring"**; and the corollary — if you could confidently predict the result, the experiment carried no information.
- The fix: **"if you measure enough different stuff, you might figure out what you're actually measuring."**
- From [How To Get Into Independent Research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/P3Yt66Wh5g7SbkKuT/how-to-get-into-independent-research-on-alignment-agency): **"Apply whatever knowledge, or combination of knowledge, you have which others in the field don't."**

#### Anthropic Fellows — [stated selection criteria](https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/anthropic-fellows-program-2026/)

- **"We care much more about your ability to execute on research than your credentials."**
- **"You don't need a PhD, prior ML experience, or published papers."**
- Three criteria: *"You can code well in Python. You can take ambiguous problems and make concrete progress"*; genuine motivation on catastrophic risk; *"You can pick up new skills quickly, debug when things break, and drive projects forward."*

Their [Recommended Directions](https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/recommended-directions/) frames the implicit question: *"What technical research can be conducted today that AI developers will find useful for ensuring the safety of their future systems?"*

#### Rohin Shah (GDM) — [career FAQ](https://rohinshah.com/faq-career-advice-for-ai-alignment-researchers/)

His fit test is essentially what you have already done: **"Spend ~50 hours reading up on a specific topic in AI alignment that interests you and ~50 hours trying to say something new and interesting about that topic."**

#### From scholars and MATS staff

- [Applying to MATS: What the Program Is Like, and Who It's For](https://www.greaterwrong.com/posts/GJWgXZ3jjYfkfzKut/applying-to-mats-what-the-program-is-like-and-who-it-s-for) (Thimmiah, Racine, Kidd, Jan 2026): *"Prior ML experience is helpful but not required."* Success traits: *"Comfort with ambiguity and open-ended problems"*, *"Willingness to revise or abandon initial ideas"*. Anti-fit: wanting *"a structured curriculum with clear assignments"* or *"an academic credential or resume signal."*
- [MATS 9 Retrospective & Advice](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eFD3rozNCZKMe4rTs/mats-9-retrospective-and-advice) (Boyd Kane, May 2026): apply to **multiple streams** *"even if you think you're not a great match"*; answer mentor Slack messages fast; and on intensity — *"most mentees were working 10-12, sometimes 14 hours a day Mon-Fri."*
- Marius Hobbhahn, [Some advice on independent research](https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/kpmaEevZ2KehZo2tp/some-advice-on-independent-research): write a 2–3 page doc answering *"what is the goal of this project?"* before doing anything.

### What a project in this programme actually looks like

**Scope correction, and it matters for how you pitch.** The unit is not a 10-week project. The main program is **12 weeks**, followed by an **optional funded 6–12 month extension that ~80% of applying fellows get**. Nearly every famous "MATS paper" is a **cohort + extension** product. A cohort-only output looks like a solid LessWrong post; the ICML/ICLR/ACL papers came after.

**Tier 1 — canonical outputs, MATS acknowledgement confirmed in the paper text:**

| Output | Fellow(s) / mentor | Scope | Link |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Refusal in LMs Is Mediated by a Single Direction** | Arditi, Obeso — Neel Nanda | One residual-stream direction ablated across **13 chat models up to 72B**. **The closest published analogue to your probe work.** | [2406.11717](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717) |
| **Ctrl-Z: Controlling AI Agents via Resampling** | Bhatt, Rushing, Tracy, Kaufman — Redwood/Shlegeris | *"the first control evaluation performed in an agent environment"*; built **BashBench, 257 multi-step tasks**. **Direct precedent for your continue/intervene/quit setup.** | [2504.10374](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10374) |
| **SHADE-Arena: Sabotage and Monitoring in LLM Agents** | Kutasov et al. — Ethan Perez | Paired benign tasks with hidden harmful side-objectives; scores sabotage *and* monitor detection. | [2506.15740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15740) |
| **AI Sandbagging** | van der Weij, Ward | Models strategically underperform on dangerous-capability evals while holding general performance. Byline affiliation is literally "MATS". | [2406.07358](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07358) |
| **SAEBench** | Karvonen, Rager, Lin — Nanda/Conmy | 8 metrics over **200+ SAEs across 8 architectures**; started in cohort, shipped in the **6.1 extension**. ICML 2025. | [2503.09532](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09532) |
| **Steering Llama 2 via Contrastive Activation Addition** | Rimsky/Panickssery — Evan Hubinger | Steering vectors from **mean residual-stream differences** over contrastive pairs. **ACL 2024 Outstanding Paper.** | [2312.06681](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.06681) |
| **Gradient Routing** | Cloud, Goldman-Wetzler — Alex Turner | Gradient masks localizing capabilities into chosen subnetworks. | [2410.04332](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.04332) |
| **On Targeted Manipulation and Deception…** | Williams — Micah Carroll | RL on simulated user feedback learns manipulation even when **only 2% of users are vulnerable**. ICLR 2025. | [2411.02306](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02306) |
| **Debating with More Persuasive LLMs** | Hughes, Valentine — Ethan Perez | Weak-to-strong oversight via debate. ICML 2024 best paper. | [2402.06782](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.06782) |
| **Emergent Misalignment** | Daniel Tan — Owain Evans | Insecure-code finetuning induces broad misalignment. **41pp, 38 figures.** ICML 2025. | [2502.17424](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424) |
| **Sparse Autoencoders Find Highly Interpretable Features** | Hoagy Cunningham | The paper that launched the SAE agenda. | [2309.08600](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08600) |
| *(cohort-only benchmark)* **Reward hacking behavior can generalize across tasks** | Nishimura-Gasparian et al. — Ethan Perez | *"produced as a part of the MATS program, advised by Ethan Perez."* LessWrong post, 87 karma. **This is what 12 weeks alone buys.** | [LessWrong](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ge55vxEmKXunFFwoe/reward-hacking-behavior-can-generalize-across-tasks) |

**Tier 2 — what the control/monitoring streams shipped in 2026**, all listed on [matsprogram.org/research](https://www.matsprogram.org/research) with named fellows. These are your nearest peers:

| Output | Fellow(s) | Link |
|---|---|---|
| Measuring Control Intervention Awareness Across Frontier LLMs | Alexander Panfilov | [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=gCzFYQuJke) |
| Adaptive Attacks on Trusted Monitors Subvert AI Control Protocols | Alexander Panfilov | [2510.09462](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09462) |
| Diffuse AI Control on Fuzzy Tasks | Mikhail Terekhov | [2606.08892](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08892) |
| How does information access affect LLM monitors' ability to detect sabotage? | Raja Moreno, Rohan Subramani | [2601.21112](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21112) |
| Building Better Activation Oracles | Jan Bauer, Celeste De Schamphelaere | [2606.02609](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02609) |
| Training Deliberative Monitors for Black-Box Scheming Detection | Akshat Naik | [2605.29601](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29601) |
| Constitutional Black-Box Monitoring for Scheming in LLM Agents | Simon Storf, Rich Barton-Cooper | [2603.00829](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00829) |
| Benchmarking and Improving Monitors for OOD Alignment Failure | Dylan Feng | [2605.21602](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21602) |
| When "Just Read the Chain of Thought" Fails | Daria Ivanova, Riya Tyagi | [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=ABy0dhxt8M) |
| Censored LLMs as a Testbed for Secret Knowledge Elicitation | Casademunt, Cywiński, Tran, Jakkli | [2603.05494](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05494) |
| Exploration Hacking: Can LLMs Learn to Resist RL Training? | Jang, Falck, Braun, Kirch | [2604.28182](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.28182) |

**Calibrate on this:** the typical unit is **one preprint, 1–4 fellows, one crisp question with ablations**. Your replication-plus-probes project is already roughly Tier-2 size. The gap is framing and a claimed finding, not scale.
